### 18. user (2026-07-06T16:49:34.473Z)

1	#!/usr/bin/env bash
2	# Create a rally GPU VM that ALWAYS installs the Ops Agent (Observability). This is THE one way to
3	# spin up a rally GPU box — it bakes the ops-agent startup-script into the VM metadata, attaches the
4	# rally-worker service account (+ cloud-platform scope so it can read/write the bucket + report
5	# metrics), and uses a driver-ready Deep-Learning-VM image (nvidia-smi works immediately).
6	#
7	# It sweeps zones for GPU capacity — asia-south1 (Mumbai, co-located with the bucket) first, then
8	# asia-southeast1 (Singapore), which L4 frequently falls back to when Mumbai is stocked-out.
9	#
10	#   bash deploy/gcp/create_gpu_vm.sh                          # L4 (default), name rally-gpu
11	#   RALLY_VM_NAME=rally-m7 RALLY_GPU=t4 bash deploy/gcp/create_gpu_vm.sh
12	#
13	# Prereq (once per project): bash deploy/gcp/provision.sh   (enables logging/monitoring + grants the
14	# SA the Ops-Agent writer roles) AND the GPUS_ALL_REGIONS quota >= 1 (README §2).
15	#
16	# ⚠ DELETE when done (a running GPU VM is the only real cost):
17	#     RALLY_VM_NAME=<name> RALLY_GCP_ZONE=<zone-it-printed> bash deploy/gcp/teardown.sh delete
18	set -uo pipefail
19	
20	# Centralized project + bucket names (single source of truth — deploy/gcp/buckets.env).
21	source "$(cd "$(dirname "${BASH_SOURCE[0]}")" && pwd)/buckets.env"
22	
23	PROJECT="$RALLY_GCP_PROJECT"
24	NAME="${RALLY_VM_NAME:-rally-gpu}"
25	SA="rally-worker@${PROJECT}.iam.gserviceaccount.com"
26	GPU="${RALLY_GPU:-l4}"     # l4 | t4
27	HERE="$(cd "$(dirname "${BASH_SOURCE[0]}")" && pwd)"
28	STARTUP="$HERE/ops-agent-startup.sh"
29	export CLOUDSDK_CORE_DISABLE_PROMPTS=1
30	
31	ZONES="${RALLY_GCP_ZONES:-asia-south1-a asia-south1-b asia-south1-c asia-southeast1-a asia-southeast1-b asia-southeast1-c}"
32	
33	if [ "$GPU" = "l4" ]; then
34	  MACHINE="g2-standard-4"; ACCEL=()          # L4 is integral to the g2 machine family
35	else
36	  MACHINE="n1-standard-8"; ACCEL=(--accelerator=type=nvidia-tesla-t4,count=1)
37	fi
38	
39	ERR="$(mktemp)"
40	for Z in $ZONES; do
41	  echo "== trying $NAME ($GPU) in $Z =="
42	  if gcloud compute instances create "$NAME" --project="$PROJECT" --zone="$Z" \
43	       --machine-type="$MACHINE" "${ACCEL[@]}" \
44	       --maintenance-policy=TERMINATE --provisioning-model=STANDARD \
45	       --image-family=common-cu129-ubuntu-2204-nvidia-580 \
46	       --image-project=deeplearning-platform-release \
47	       --boot-disk-size=120GB --boot-disk-type=pd-balanced \
48	       --service-account="$SA" --scopes=cloud-platform \
49	       --metadata-from-file=startup-script="$STARTUP" 2>"$ERR"; then
50	    echo "✅ CREATED $NAME in $Z — the Ops Agent installs on boot (Observability ~2-3 min later)."
51	    echo "   SSH:    gcloud compute ssh $NAME --zone=$Z"
52	    echo "   DELETE: RALLY_VM_NAME=$NAME RALLY_GCP_ZONE=$Z bash deploy/gcp/teardown.sh delete"
53	    rm -f "$ERR"; exit 0
54	  fi
55	  if grep -qiE "STOCKOUT|does not have enough" "$ERR"; then
56	    echo "  (no $GPU capacity in $Z — trying the next zone)"; continue
57	  fi
58	  echo "  create failed:"; tail -4 "$ERR"; rm -f "$ERR"; exit 1
59	done
60	echo "❌ No $GPU capacity in any swept zone ($ZONES). Try again later or RALLY_GPU=t4."
61	rm -f "$ERR"; exit 1
62

### 20. user (2026-07-06T16:49:41.558Z)

1	# shellcheck shell=bash
2	# Single source of truth for the GCP deploy layer's PROJECT + BUCKET names.
3	# Every deploy/gcp script `source`s this, so a rename — or standing up a NEW deployment / region — is
4	# ONE edit here, not a hunt across scripts. Override any value via the environment before invoking a
5	# script (these are just the defaults; a caller-set env value always wins).
6	#
7	# DATA-FAMILY SEPARATION (never conflate — this is a compliance boundary, not tidiness):
8	#   * TRAINING CORPUS   — golden clips/labels + WASB weights, PERMANENT (no TTL)   -> RALLY_CORPUS_BUCKET
9	#   * NIGHTLY/dev OUTPUT — reports, repo tarball, Cloud Build staging, 30-day TTL  -> RALLY_NIGHTLY_BUCKET
10	#   * USER-UPLOADED videos are a SEPARATE surface entirely — the engine's StorageConfig
11	#     (backend/config/models.py) and the per-request `medium_uri` of POST /api/assets — and must NEVER
12	#     land in these buckets. Keep them physically apart so consent / no-minors / not-training holds at
13	#     the storage layer, not on a promise.
14	#
15	# PER-DEPLOYMENT / PER-REGION: a regional deploy just sets RALLY_GCP_PROJECT + RALLY_CORPUS_BUCKET
16	# (+ RALLY_NIGHTLY_BUCKET) to its own values (e.g. an EU box -> gs://khelsutra-corpus-eu). No script
17	# or code change — that is the whole point of centralizing the names here.
18	
19	# GCP project every bucket + the worker service account live under.
20	export RALLY_GCP_PROJECT="${RALLY_GCP_PROJECT:-gen-lang-client-0306026826}"
21	
22	# Training corpus (permanent, no TTL). Canonical name = RALLY_CORPUS_BUCKET. The two legacy names that
23	# used to be typed independently — RALLY_GCS_BUCKET (provision.sh) and RALLY_BUCKET (launch.sh) — are
24	# still honored for back-compat, then re-exported to the canonical value so any old invocation OR a
25	# script still reading either name resolves to the SAME bucket (the divergence that caused drift).
26	export RALLY_CORPUS_BUCKET="${RALLY_CORPUS_BUCKET:-${RALLY_GCS_BUCKET:-${RALLY_BUCKET:-khelsutra-rally-corpus}}}"
27	export RALLY_GCS_BUCKET="$RALLY_CORPUS_BUCKET"   # back-compat alias (was provision.sh's variable)
28	export RALLY_BUCKET="$RALLY_CORPUS_BUCKET"       # back-compat alias (was launch.sh's variable)
29	
30	# Nightly / dev EPHEMERAL outputs (30-day TTL; isolated from the corpus so a bucket-wide TTL can never
31	# touch golden data — that blast-radius isolation is deliberate).
32	export RALLY_NIGHTLY_BUCKET="${RALLY_NIGHTLY_BUCKET:-khelsutra-rally-nightly}"
33	
34	# WASB weights object. Lives UNDER the corpus bucket, so it is derived from RALLY_CORPUS_BUCKET (a
35	# corpus rename carries the weights path with it); override RALLY_WEIGHTS_URI to point elsewhere.
36	export RALLY_WEIGHTS_URI="${RALLY_WEIGHTS_URI:-gs://${RALLY_CORPUS_BUCKET}/models/wasb_badminton_best.pth.tar}"
37

### 22. user (2026-07-06T16:49:55.487Z)

1	#!/usr/bin/env bash
2	# Switch OFF the billable GCP resources between sessions (cost control). The GPU VM is the only
3	# meaningful cost. ⚠ A STOPPED VM STILL BILLS FOR ITS DISK (pd-ssd ≈ $0.17/GB/mo → a 200 GB boot
4	# disk ≈ $34/mo) — only DELETE takes it to $0. So DELETE is the DEFAULT here (owner cost rule,
5	# 2026-06-20: "make sure VMs are deleting, not just stopped"); use `stop` only for a deliberate
6	# fast-resume pause. The GCS bucket is NEVER auto-deleted (it holds the corpus) — drop it manually.
7	#
8	# Usage:
9	#   bash deploy/gcp/teardown.sh            # DELETE the VM (default; $0 — removes VM + boot disk)
10	#   bash deploy/gcp/teardown.sh delete     # delete the VM (no disk cost; full re-port to resume)
11	#   bash deploy/gcp/teardown.sh stop       # STOP only — keeps the disk → STILL BILLS (~$/mo!); fast resume
12	#   bash deploy/gcp/teardown.sh all        # delete VM + SA + budget (keeps the bucket/data)
13	#
14	# Cross-region VM: set the zone, e.g.  RALLY_GCP_ZONE=asia-southeast1-a bash deploy/gcp/teardown.sh
15	#
16	# VM name: pass RALLY_VM_NAME=<name> — the SAME var create_gpu_vm.sh provisions with (e.g.
17	# RALLY_VM_NAME=khelsutra-gpu). RALLY_GPU_VM is kept as a backward-compatible alias. If neither is set
18	# the name defaults to rally-gpu, so a CUSTOM-named VM is NOT deleted unless you pass its name — the
19	# command would otherwise target a non-existent VM, exit cleanly, and leave the real one billing.
20	set -uo pipefail
21	
22	# Centralized project + bucket names (single source of truth — deploy/gcp/buckets.env).
23	source "$(cd "$(dirname "${BASH_SOURCE[0]}")" && pwd)/buckets.env"
24	
25	PROJECT="$RALLY_GCP_PROJECT"
26	ZONE="${RALLY_GCP_ZONE:-asia-south1-a}"
27	# RALLY_VM_NAME (canonical, matches create_gpu_vm.sh) > RALLY_GPU_VM (legacy alias) > default.
28	VM="${RALLY_VM_NAME:-${RALLY_GPU_VM:-rally-gpu}}"
29	SA_EMAIL="rally-worker@${PROJECT}.iam.gserviceaccount.com"
30	BILLING_ACCT="${RALLY_BILLING_ACCT:-01420D-419EE0-53D83C}"
31	export CLOUDSDK_CORE_DISABLE_PROMPTS=1
32	ACTION="${1:-delete}"
33	
34	case "$ACTION" in
35	  stop)
36	    echo "== stopping $VM — ⚠ the DISK STILL BILLS (~\$/mo); use 'delete' for \$0. Resume: gcloud compute instances start $VM --zone=$ZONE =="
37	    gcloud compute instances stop "$VM" --zone="$ZONE" --project "$PROJECT" || echo "  (no running VM named $VM)"
38	    ;;
39	  delete)
40	    echo "== deleting $VM (VM + boot disk autoDelete=true => \$0; full re-port to resume) =="
41	    gcloud compute instances delete "$VM" --zone="$ZONE" --project "$PROJECT" --quiet || echo "  (no VM named $VM)"
42	    echo "  audit orphans: gcloud compute disks list --project $PROJECT   (expect NO unattached disk — it would still bill)"
43	    ;;
44	  all)
45	    echo "== deleting VM + SA + budget (bucket/data kept) =="
46	    gcloud compute instances delete "$VM" --zone="$ZONE" --project "$PROJECT" --quiet || echo "  (no VM)"
47	    gcloud iam service-accounts delete "$SA_EMAIL" --project "$PROJECT" --quiet || echo "  (no SA)"
48	    bid=$(gcloud billing budgets list --billing-account="$BILLING_ACCT" \
49	          --filter="displayName=rally-100usd-guard" --format="value(name)" 2>/dev/null | head -1)
50	    [ -n "$bid" ] && gcloud billing budgets delete "$bid" --billing-account="$BILLING_ACCT" --quiet || echo "  (no budget)"

### 37. assistant (2026-07-06T16:53:50.317Z)

Good — that's intentional by design, not drift. Here's the complete Cloud Run picture.

## Cloud Run: 1 service, 1 running instance (always-warm by design)

`gcloud run services list` covers all regions — there's exactly **one** service:

| | |
|---|---|
| **Service** | `rally-control-plane` |
| **Region** | `asia-southeast1` (Singapore) |
| **Status** | ✔ Ready — 100% traffic on latest revision |
| **Serving revision** | `rally-control-plane-00015-kp9` (deployed 2026-07-05 15:22 UTC by you) |
| **Resources** | 2 vCPU / 8 GiB |
| **URL** | https://rally-control-plane-2bosgeukpa-as.a.run.app |

**"How many are there" — depends on the axis:**
- **Services:** 1
- **Running container instances:** exactly **1**, always. `minScale=1` **and** `maxScale=1` are both pinned, with `cpu-throttling=false` → one instance stays warm with CPU always allocated.
- **Revisions:** 15 total (`00001` → `00015`), but only `00015-kp9` is active/serving. The other 14 are inactive deployment history — they don't run and don't bill.

### The one billing note worth flagging
Unlike the GPU VM (which is at **$0** — deleted), this Cloud Run service **bills continuously**: `minScale=1` + `--no-cpu-throttling` means one 2‑vCPU/8Gi instance is always warm, 24/7. 

That is **intentional, not drift** — [gen_service_yaml.py](../khelsutra-guru/badminton-highlight-indexer/deploy/cloudrun/gen_service_yaml.py) and the [README](../khelsutra-guru/badminton-highlight-indexer/deploy/cloudrun/README.md) document it: an in-process job-worker drain thread needs CPU between requests (without it, `/api/compile` returns 202 then stalls), and `maxScale=1` is pinned because the jobs/videos DB is per-instance ephemeral SQLite, so a second instance would split state. The README explicitly notes this is *"cheap CPU; NOT the D7 GPU pool."* So it's the intended cost floor of the control plane, but it is a nonzero always-on cost — worth knowing if you thought the project was fully idle.

Cross-check: serving revision `00015-kp9` matches the latest `master` commit I saw on pull (`rev 00015-kp9`), so the deployed control plane is current with the repo.

### 58. assistant (2026-07-06T17:00:38.027Z)

I traced the full processing run. Here's what happened — and it's a **silent failure**: the job reported success but produced **zero highlights**.

## What happened to "All 1 v 1.MP4"

First, an important reframing of my earlier answer: **this is your GPU serving path.** The processing ran on the Cloud Run **Job** `rally-worker` *with a GPU* (`torch 1.11.0+cu113, cuda True`), not on the `rally-gpu` Compute Engine VM (which is still deleted/$0). The GPU serving is on-demand via this Cloud Run Job, which is why `instances list` showed nothing — the GPU only exists for the ~32 min the job runs.

**Execution `rally-worker-wf294`** — 2026-07-06 16:17:08 → 16:49:16 UTC (~32 min), Cloud Run status ✔ succeeded.

| Time (UTC) | Event |
|---|---|
| 16:14:17 | Alpha user's `All 1 v 1.MP4` (**2.12 GiB**) landed in `gs://khelsutra-rally-serving/videos/uploads/d2d89b44…/` |
| 16:17:17 | Job mounted the serving bucket (GCSFuse) |
| 16:18:02 | Pulled the 2.12 GiB video → `/tmp/…/in.mp4` |
| 16:18:52 | WASB segmenter started in **fully-local mode** (`skip_ai_handoff=True` — no Gemini, pure shuttle-trajectory) |
| 16:19:06 | Native WASB inference started on GPU (`device=cuda`) |
| **16:49:07** | **❌ `WASB native inference timed out after 1800s`** → "WASB produced no trajectory; aborting" |
| 16:49:07 | `infer produced 0 segments`; wrote `intervals.csv` (empty) + `report.json`; wrote `.done` marker; exit 0 |

**Outcome:** `report.json` says `"status": "success"` but `"segment_count": 0` / `"segments": []`, and `intervals.csv` is just a header. **The alpha user got no rallies/highlights at all.**

## Root cause
The shuttle-detector (WASB native CUDA inference) hit its **30-minute cap** — `indexing.wasb.timeout_sec: 1800` in [config.json:64](../khelsutra-guru/badminton-highlight-indexer/config.json) — and was killed mid-run. The **2.12 GiB** input was simply too long/large to finish trajectory detection within 1800s on this GPU. Inference ran the full 30 minutes (16:19:06 → 16:49:07) and produced nothing before being terminated. When WASB yields no trajectory, the segmenter aborts → 0 segments. See [native_wasb_runner.py:81](../khelsutra-guru/badminton-highlight-indexer/backend/pipeline/detectors/native_wasb_runner.py).

## The part worth worrying about 🔴
The worker treats a **timeout-with-zero-output as `status: "success"`**. So the control plane, the completion marker, and the alpha user's result surface all see a clean "done" — the failure is invisible unless you read the logs (as we just did). For an alpha launch, a video that silently returns empty highlights is worse than a visible error. I've flagged this as a separate task.

## How to actually fix the processing
Concrete levers, roughly in order of leverage:
1. **Raise `wasb.timeout_sec`** for serving (e.g. 3600s+) — but that just trades a silent empty result for a longer, more expensive run; not sufficient alone.
2. **Speed up inference** so it fits the budget: the WASB GPU-feed tuning (`batch_size`/`prefetch_batches`) isn't set in the serving `wasb` config block (so it's at the slow defaults), and/or **downscale to the tuned proxy resolution** before inference — a 2.12 GiB clip at full res is the expensive path.
3. **Chunk long videos** into segments the detector can finish within the cap.
4. **Fix the status semantics** (the flagged bug) so a timeout reports `failed`/`partial`, not `success`.

Want me to dig into which of these is the real bottleneck — e.g. pull the video's duration/resolution and check whether the serving config is skipping the proxy-downscale + batch tuning? That would tell us whether to speed up inference vs. just raise the cap.

### 68. user (2026-07-06T17:08:48.496Z)

############## ISSUE #483 FULL (json) ##############
TITLE: [cost] Downsample 'detectably long' uploads to a fast proxy for DETECTION (GPU ffmpeg); build the reel from the ORIGINAL
STATE: OPEN
LABELS: 

===BODY===
## Idea (owner, 2026-07-05) — save GPU cost while the product is free

Rally **detection** is the expensive GPU step and does **not** need full resolution/fps. For long / high-fps uploads it dominates cost and blows past our timeouts (a 1080p**60** ~2.7 GB clip on 2026-07-05 exceeded both the SPA 10-min poll and the CP 30-min `max_wait`). The final **reel**, however, must be full quality.

## Proposal

1. **Detect "long-running" uploads** up front — by duration / frame-count / estimated-processing-time crossing a threshold (we can estimate from res·fps·duration and the known ~25 fps WASB throughput on L4).
2. **Transcode a downsampled proxy** — lower resolution + fps (e.g. 720p/30, or lower) — using **GPU-accelerated ffmpeg (NVDEC/NVENC)** on the worker (cheap + fast).
3. **Run WASB rally detection on the PROXY** — far less GPU time.
4. **Map detected rally timestamps back to the ORIGINAL** and **compile the final reel from the original** (full quality, no re-detection).
5. **Tell the user**: e.g. *"This is a long video — we're finding rallies on a fast downsampled copy to keep things quick; your reel will be cut from the original full-quality video."*

## Guardrail

Verify detection quality on the proxy doesn't materially regress — run the golden set at the chosen proxy res/fps and compare F1/tolF1 before enabling. Pick the most aggressive downsample that holds quality.

## Payoff

Big GPU-cost reduction on long uploads (the common real-match case), keeps latency sane, and removes the "video too long for the timeouts" failure mode — all while the reel stays full quality. Pairs with the timeout fixes (SPA poll ceiling, CP `max_wait`) and #331/#349 (perf).

### 81. assistant (2026-07-06T17:16:22.523Z)

All three explorations are in. The perf model (agent 1) reveals the decisive mechanism and a bonus lever:

1. **WASB processes *every* frame (sliding window, stride=1) — no fps-skip knob.** So inference time is driven by **frame count**. Halving fps (60→30) ≈ halves time. This is the dominant, reliable lever.
2. **GPU forward-pass cost is *independent* of source resolution** (frames are resized to a fixed network input). Spatial downscale (1080→720) only cuts **CPU decode/resize** cost — but the logs note **GPU was <1% utilized at batch=8/prefetch=0**, meaning the pipeline is *CPU-loader-bound*. So 720p **and** `batch_size↑`/`prefetch_batches↑` should raise throughput **with zero change to which frames are detected** (identical detection → no quality gate needed for the tuning lever).

That gives three testable levers. My experiment will measure all of them on a real L4 and prove end-to-end completion. Let me record the plan and spin up the GPU box (co-located with the serving bucket in asia-southeast1).

Here's my plan before I start spending GPU:

**Experiment (one L4 VM in `asia-southeast1`, torn down after):**
| Run | Variant | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| Baseline | 1080p60, batch=8/prefetch=0, timeout off | Confirm ~25 fps + get **true full-video segment count** (quality baseline; the prod run got 0 because it aborted) |
| Lever C | 1080p60, batch↑/prefetch↑ | Speedup with **identical** detection (no quality risk) |
| Lever A | **1080p30** proxy | ~½ frames → time + segments vs baseline |
| Lever A+B | **720p30** proxy | fps + res → time + segments vs baseline |
| Capstone | Winning proxy through the **real Cloud Run Job** | Prove the production path completes <1800s |

### 96. assistant (2026-07-06T17:23:12.119Z)

---
name: efficient-timeouts-and-retries
description: How the owner wants Claude to handle waits, retries, timeouts, and Windows gcloud SSH — fast, visible, diagnostic
metadata:
  type: feedback
---

The owner strongly dislikes slow, silent retry loops. On 2026-07-06, diagnosing a GPU VM, I burned ~3 minutes on a 12×15s SSH-readiness loop that suppressed stderr with `2>/dev/null`, so it "failed" invisibly (SSH_READY=0) while the owner had already SSH'd into the box manually a minute earlier. The owner interrupted repeatedly to force this correction.

**Why:** wasteful polling plus hidden errors makes me look slow and not smart; the owner watches actively and will interrupt. A single visible failure that I diagnose beats many blind retries.

**How to apply:**
- Prefer ONE attempt with visible stderr, read the real error, then fix — do not blind-retry a broken command.
- Use short, reasonable timeouts (seconds, not minutes). Escalate the timeout only with a concrete reason.
- Never hide stderr (`2>/dev/null`) on a command whose success is uncertain.
- The real root cause here: on Windows, `gcloud compute ssh` shells out to PuTTY `plink.exe`, which does NOT understand OpenSSH `-o` flags. Both `--ssh-flag="-o ..."` and `-- -o ...` fail with `plink: unknown option "-o"`. Use plain `gcloud compute ssh <vm> --zone=<z> --project=<p> --command="..."` with NO `-o` flags. The key (`google_compute_engine`) and known_hosts are already set up.
- For long remote work, run it detached on the VM (nohup/tmux, write progress to a file or GCS) and poll the status file, instead of many SSH round-trips.

### 100. assistant (2026-07-06T17:26:10.288Z)

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Diag #483: make 1080p30 + 720p30 detection proxies of "All 1 v 1.MP4" and upload to serving bucket.
set -uo pipefail
mkdir -p ~/diag && cd ~/diag
LOG=~/diag/transcode.log
: > "$LOG"
exec > >(tee -a "$LOG") 2>&1
echo "=== $(date -u) START transcode ==="
SRC="gs://khelsutra-rally-serving/videos/uploads/d2d89b44d9e0497a97ca9a6c8ce4a19d/All 1 v 1.MP4"

if ! command -v ffmpeg >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  echo "[setup] installing ffmpeg..."
  sudo apt-get update -qq && sudo DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive apt-get install -y -qq ffmpeg
fi
ffmpeg -hide_banner -version | head -1

echo "[dl] downloading original (2.12 GiB)..."
time gsutil -q cp "$SRC" in.mp4
echo "[probe] source:"; ffprobe -v error -select_streams v:0 -show_entries stream=width,height,r_frame_rate,nb_frames,codec_name -of default in.mp4

NVENC=""
if ffmpeg -hide_banner -encoders 2>/dev/null | grep -q h264_nvenc; then NVENC=1; echo "[enc] h264_nvenc AVAILABLE (GPU transcode)"; else echo "[enc] no nvenc; using libx264 (CPU)"; fi
ENCARGS(){ if [ -n "$NVENC" ]; then echo "-c:v h264_nvenc -preset p5 -cq 23"; else echo "-c:v libx264 -preset veryfast -crf 20"; fi; }

echo "=== [t1] 1080p30 ==="
time ffmpeg -y -hide_banner -loglevel error -i in.mp4 -vf "fps=30" $(ENCARGS) -c:a copy p1080p30.mp4
echo "=== [t2] 720p30 ==="
time ffmpeg -y -hide_banner -loglevel error -i in.mp4 -vf "fps=30,scale=1280:720" $(ENCARGS) -c:a copy p720p30.mp4

ls -la in.mp4 p1080p30.mp4 p720p30.mp4
for f in p1080p30 p720p30; do echo "[probe] $f:"; ffprobe -v error -select_streams v:0 -show_entries stream=width,height,r_frame_rate,nb_frames -of default $f.mp4; done

### 103. assistant (2026-07-06T17:26:41.169Z)

---
name: efficient-timeouts-and-retries
description: How the owner wants Claude to handle waits, retries, timeouts, and Windows gcloud SSH — fast, visible, diagnostic
metadata:
  type: feedback
---

The owner strongly dislikes slow, silent retry loops. On 2026-07-06, diagnosing a GPU VM, I burned ~3 minutes on a 12×15s SSH-readiness loop that suppressed stderr with `2>/dev/null`, so it "failed" invisibly (SSH_READY=0) while the owner had already SSH'd into the box manually a minute earlier. The owner interrupted repeatedly to force this correction ("be really smart at your job").

**Why:** wasteful polling plus hidden errors makes me look slow and not smart; the owner watches actively and will interrupt. One visible failure I actually diagnose beats many blind retries.

**How to apply:**
- Prefer ONE attempt with visible stderr, read the real error, then fix — do not blind-retry a broken command.
- Use short, reasonable timeouts (seconds, not minutes). Escalate only with a concrete reason.
- Never hide stderr (`2>/dev/null`) on a command whose success is uncertain.
- Root cause of that incident: on Windows, `gcloud compute ssh` shells out to PuTTY `plink.exe`, which does NOT understand OpenSSH `-o` flags. Both `--ssh-flag="-o ..."` and `-- -o ...` fail with `plink: unknown option "-o"`. Use plain `gcloud compute ssh <vm> --zone=<z> --project=<p> --command="..."` with NO `-o` flags. The key (`google_compute_engine`) and known_hosts are already set up; plink may prompt once to cache the host key but still runs the command.
- For long remote work, run it detached on the VM (nohup, write progress to a log/GCS) and poll the status file, instead of many SSH round-trips.

### 121. assistant (2026-07-06T17:34:30.428Z)

> Investigation opened from a diagnostic session on the alpha timeout of "All 1 v 1.MP4" (1080p60, 46,080 frames, 12.8 min), which hit the 1800s WASB cap and returned 0 segments. Filed to decide later. Companion to the downsample-proxy work in #483 (they compose).

## Problem

WASB shuttle-trajectory detection is the pipeline's expensive step: one GPU forward per sliding window (`frames_in` wide, stride 1) over **every** frame (`build_windows`, `backend/pipeline/detectors/wasb_infer.py:257-266`). Throughput is ~25 fps on an L4 and the pipeline is **CPU-loader-bound**, not GPU-bound (GPU measured <1% utilized at batch=8/prefetch=0 — see `_PrefetchIter`, `backend/pipeline/detectors/wasb_infer.py:403-413`, and #349).

This caps how long a single upload can be. The alpha clip **"All 1 v 1.MP4"** (1920×1080, 59.94 fps, 46,080 frames, 12.8 min) needs ~1843s of native detection but WASB self-caps at `indexing.wasb.timeout_sec=1800` (`backend/config/models.py`; enforced as the subprocess timeout at `backend/pipeline/detectors/native_wasb_runner.py:140` → logged at `native_wasb_runner.py:306-310`). It **timed out and produced 0 segments** while `report.json` still read `status:"success", segment_count:0`. The Cloud Run Job task timeout is 3600s and is **not** the binding limit — the 1800s WASB cap is.

We have a single-machine, single-GPU serial pipeline. There is no lever today to put **one long video** across **multiple GPUs** to cut wall-clock.

## Idea

Split one video into K time-chunks, detect them **in parallel** on K workers, then merge the per-chunk rally intervals back into one result. Rally timestamps are in **seconds** and the reel is cut from the ORIGINAL, so per-chunk detections compose by adding each chunk's start offset — no resolution/fps bookkeeping.

The repo is already primed for this:

- **The windowing engine is chunk-aware.** `trajectory_to_action_windows` takes `chunk_start` + `chunk_index` and re-bases every window by `chunk_start`. WASB just always passes `chunk_start=0.0` (`backend/pipeline/segmenters/trajectory_hybrid.py:316`).
- **A per-chunk split + fan-in/merge already ships** in `yolo_hybrid._collect_candidate_windows` (`backend/pipeline/segmenters/yolo_hybrid.py:108-262`): split via `extract_video_chunk` with `chunk_overlap`, process each chunk with `chunk_start`/`chunk_index`, then dedup-merge cross-chunk windows by sort-then-merge-on-overlap (`:231-257`). Directly reusable as the fan-in.
- **The frame-range decode primitive exists.** `make_video_frame_loader` seeks with `cap.set(cv2.CAP_PROP_POS_FRAMES, index)` (`backend/pipeline/detectors/wasb_infer.py:772`) and `SequentialFrameStore` already takes a `start_index` (`:283-291`) — both used for resume today. Only "stop after M frames" is missing beyond `--limit` (which slices from the start only, `:522-523`).

## Two architectures

### A. Pre-split source into K sub-clips (recommended for v1)

Control plane: `ffmpeg` the source into K overlapping sub-clips (reuse `extract_video_chunk`, `backend/utils/video.py:51`), upload each to GCS, dispatch K independent `rally-worker` executions (one per sub-clip) via the **existing** `CloudRunCompute.run_infer` path (`backend/compute/cloud_run.py:179-246`), bucket-poll all K done-markers, then merge the K `intervals.csv` with a start-offset re-base + the yolo_hybrid overlap dedup, and ingest via `_ingest_remote_segments` (`backend/orchestration.py:108-155`).

- **Pros:** Zero change to the worker, `wasb_infer.py`, or the WASB timeout. Each sub-clip run is **byte-identical to a standalone run** — no new correctness surface inside the detector. Reuses dispatch + done-marker + ingest wholesale. Naturally fault-isolated (one bad chunk fails alone).
- **Cons:** Extra transcode/split + K uploads of sub-clips. K separate executions = K cold-starts + K dispatch/poll cycles. Orchestration must live above `_maybe_dispatch_remote` (`backend/orchestration.py:391`), which today does one dispatch per video.

### B. Single execution, `taskCount=K`, shard-by-index (follow-up)

One Cloud Run Job execution with K tasks; each task reads `CLOUD_RUN_TASK_INDEX` to pick its `[start_frame, start_frame+count)` shard (with halo), runs WASB over that range, writes a partial `intervals.csv`, and the last task (or a separate merge job) fans in.

- **Pros:** One dispatch, one execution; no sub-clip transcode/upload (each task slices by frame range from the same source object). Cheaper dispatch overhead.
- **Cons:** **Does not exist today.** `run_job` fires a *single* execution with container-arg overrides (`backend/compute/cloud_run.py:110-123`); there is no `--tasks`/`taskCount` or `CLOUD_RUN_TASK_INDEX` handling anywhere. Needs: (1) a `--start-frame`/`--frame-count` (or `--start-sec`/`--dur-sec`) capability on `infer` → `NativeWasbRunner.run_predict` → `wasb_infer.py` (extending the existing `--limit` and `start_index` seams at `wasb_infer.py:522-523,283-291,772`); (2) task-index sharding in `backend/worker/__main__.py:cmd_infer`; (3) a fan-in "last task merges" contract + per-shard output naming; (4) partial-failure semantics across tasks.

**Recommendation: ship A first** (reuses the whole existing decoupled-compute path; smallest, lowest-risk change). Treat **B as a fast-follow** once A proves the merge math on the golden set.

## Correctness (boundary halo + merge/dedup)

- **Chunk with a halo overlap.** Cut chunk *i* as `[i·step - halo, i·step + chunk_len]` where `step = chunk_len - halo`, mirroring yolo_hybrid's `step = chunk_duration - chunk_overlap` (`backend/pipeline/segmenters/yolo_hybrid.py:123`). Size `halo` ≥ the longest plausible rally boundary uncertainty (start ~2s). A rally straddling a cut then appears whole in at least one chunk (or as two overlapping halves the merge rejoins).
- **The WASB sliding window is NOT a cross-chunk risk.** Each sub-clip decodes independently; `build_windows` only drops `frames_in-1` windows at each clip's own edges (a few frames, <100ms at 60fps), fully absorbed by the halo. No need to stitch detector state across chunks.
- **Merge/dedup.** Re-base each chunk's window times by its `chunk_start` (already done inside `trajectory_to_action_windows`), then apply the yolo_hybrid **sort-then-merge-on-overlap** dedup (`yolo_hybrid.py:248-257`). For the fan-in of serialized `intervals.csv` rows, apply the same overlap-merge on `(start,end)` (optionally IoU-gated) before/at ingest.
- **Validation gate (must pass before rollout):** for a fixed clip, assert `merge(chunked K-pass) == single-pass` within tolerance on the golden set — same rally set, boundaries within a few frames. Sweep `halo`; confirm no rally dropped or double-counted at any seam.

## Cost / latency (46,080 frames @ ~25 fps, serial ≈ 1843s)

Wall-clock ≈ `1843/K + overhead` (transcode/split + K sub-clip uploads + cold start + merge). GPU-seconds total is **unchanged** (K× concurrent L4 for 1/K the time). Overhead below is a rough placeholder — measure on a real run.

| K | detect wall-clock (1843/K) | + overhead (est.) | ≈ total | concurrent L4 | vs 1800s cap |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1843s | — | 1843s | 1 | **exceeds → 0 segments (today)** |
| 2 | 922s | ~90s | ~1010s | 2 | under |
| 4 | 461s | ~120s | ~580s | 4 | well under |
| 8 | 230s | ~150s | ~380s | 8 | well under |

**GPU quota:** K concurrent executions need **K concurrent L4** in the serving region (Cloud Run GPU = 1 GPU/instance).

## Relationship to #483, #331, #349

- **#483 (proxy downsample) — COMPOSES, orthogonal.** #483 reduces **total work** (fewer effective frames via a 1080p30/720p30 proxy; note GPU forward cost is source-resolution-independent, so its real win is fps/frame-count reduction). Chunking reduces **wall-clock** at constant total work. Best result: proxy for detection **and** chunk the proxy. Timestamps are in seconds either way; reel is cut from the ORIGINAL.
- **#331 (hosted processing is CPU-bound).** A concrete instance of #331's "parallelize extraction/async" lever — across machines rather than speeding up one machine.
- **#349 (WASB decode CPU-bound/single-threaded → L4 idle).** #349 attacks the same bottleneck *within* one worker (NVDEC/parallel decode); chunking is the *across-worker* complement, and #349 makes each chunk cheaper. Compose.

## Open questions

- **`halo` size:** what overlap rejoins every straddling rally with zero double-counts? (Sweep on the golden set — the merge-correctness gate.)
- **Where does split/merge orchestration live** — a new control-plane path beside `_maybe_dispatch_remote` (`backend/orchestration.py:391`), or a chunk-aware segmenter variant? (A only needs orchestration; B needs worker changes.)
- **Partial-chunk failure policy:** if 1 of K chunks fails/times out, fail the whole video or serve K-1 with a gap flag? (Honor `_ingest_remote_segments` / `_finalize_reingest` destroy-after-confirm semantics.)
- **Sub-clip identity vs the md5 join key:** each sub-clip has its own md5, so the worker's `compute_video_id` differs per chunk. The merge must ingest under the **parent** video_id (parallels the existing "cloud video_id != local" reconcile at `backend/orchestration.py:573-581`).
- **GPU quota / cost ceiling:** is K concurrent L4 within quota, and is K× concurrent GPU spend acceptable? (Total GPU-seconds unchanged; peak concurrent cost is K×.)
- **Merge overhead is unmeasured** — the overhead column is an estimate; phase 1 should measure split + upload + cold-start on a real run.
- **Architecture B mechanics:** does the SDK path (`run_v2.RunJobRequest`) accept a task-count override alongside `container_overrides`? (Not exercised in code today — `backend/compute/cloud_run.py:110-118`.)

## Suggested phased plan

1. **Measure + validate the merge math (no infra).** Locally split "All 1 v 1.MP4" into K overlapping sub-clips (`extract_video_chunk`), run WASB (`detector_impl=native`) on each, re-base by `chunk_start`, merge via the yolo_hybrid overlap dedup, and assert `merged == single-pass` within tolerance on the golden set. Sweep `halo`. **Gate:** merge correctness proven before any dispatch work.
2. **Architecture A orchestration.** Add a control-plane fan-out beside `_maybe_dispatch_remote` (`backend/orchestration.py:391-631`): split + upload K sub-clips, K× `CloudRunCompute.run_infer`, poll K done-markers, merge K `intervals.csv`, ingest under the parent video_id. Config-gated + default-OFF; trigger only when `estimated_detect_sec > timeout_sec` (or an explicit length threshold). **No** change to `wasb_infer.py` / `native_wasb_runner.py` / the WASB timeout.
3. **Compose with #483.** Detect on the proxy AND chunk the proxy; verify timestamps still map to the ORIGINAL for the reel cut. Confirm the combined path clears the cap with margin on the alpha clip.
4. **(Fast-follow) Architecture B.** Add `--start-frame`/`--frame-count` (or `--start-sec`/`--dur-sec`) to `infer` → `run_predict` → `wasb_infer.py`, `CLOUD_RUN_TASK_INDEX` sharding in `cmd_infer` (`backend/worker/__main__.py`), and a `taskCount=K` dispatch path in `CloudRunJobClient.run_job` (`backend/compute/cloud_run.py:84-123`) with a designated merge task. Removes the sub-clip transcode/upload + K cold-starts.

### 129. assistant (2026-07-06T17:36:58.897Z)

Both waiters are running server-side (I'll be notified — no foreground polling). Here's where things stand so you can see the shape while the GPU work runs:

## Status

**Diagnosis (proven from code + the real execution logs):**
- `All 1 v 1.MP4` = **1080p, 59.94 fps, 46,080 frames, 12.8 min**.
- WASB processes **every** frame (sliding window, stride=1 — no fps-skip). At the documented **~25 fps on L4**: `46,080 ÷ 25 ≈ 1,843 s` vs the **1,800 s** `indexing.wasb.timeout_sec` cap → **it timed out by ~43 s (~2.3%), after finishing ~97% of the video.** Razor-thin.
- Three independent speed levers, each code-grounded:
  - **fps 60→30** → halves frames → ~halves time (dominant, sanctioned by #483).
  - **1080→720** → cuts the CPU-decode cost (the pipeline is *loader-bound*: GPU measured <1% utilized).
  - **batch/prefetch tuning** → raises GPU utilization with **identical detection** (no quality gate).

**Deliverables landed:**
- 🐛 Silent-success bug → spawned task (you started it).
- 📋 Chunking/parallel proposal → **filed as [#500](https://github.com/Khelsutra/badminton-highlight-indexer/issues/500)** (the research found the repo already has chunk-aware windowing + a working split/merge in `yolo_hybrid` — so it's a small, low-risk change).
- 🧠 Registered your feedback to memory (no silent retry loops; the plink/`-o` root cause).

**In flight on the real L4 serving path:**
| Run | State |
|---|---|
| Transcode → `1080p30` + `720p30` proxies (on the VM) | encoding |
| Baseline throughput (`8tmr7`, original, 9k frames) | running |
| **720p30 proxy → prove completion <1800s + segments** | queued (needs proxy) |
| 1080p30 proxy + original-tuned (lever C) | queued |

The money-shot is the 720p30 proxy run: I expect ~**920 s** (≈51% of budget) with non-zero rallies. Once the waiters report, I'll fire the proxy runs and assemble the data-grounded proposal to post on **#483**. I'll delete the VM when done.

1	#!/usr/bin/env bash
2	#
3	# Native (no-WSL) WASB shuttle-detector setup for a Linux GPU box — the M3.5 enabler for
4	# real trajectory extraction. Replicates docs/TRACKNET_WSL_SETUP.md's WSL conda env
5	# *natively* on Linux. Run AFTER ./bootstrap.sh --gpu (which provides CUDA torch for the
6	# main venv); this builds a SEPARATE conda env for WASB (torch 1.11) — never co-installed
7	# with the indexer's torch (framework conflict).
8	#
9	# Requires an NVIDIA GPU + driver (the DO `gpu-*-base` image ships it).
10	#
11	# ⚠ WASB-C3: the pretrained badminton weights' license / source-dataset provenance is NOT
12	# verified here. Fine for internal extraction + Gen-0 bootstrapping; clear it before sharing
13	# any derived trajectories or model. See docs/DECOUPLED_COMPUTE/06.
14	set -euo pipefail
15	
16	MODELS_DIR="${RALLY_MODELS_DIR:-$HOME/models}"
17	CONDA_DIR="${CONDA_DIR:-$HOME/miniconda3}"
18	WASB_REPO="$MODELS_DIR/WASB-SBDT"
19	mkdir -p "$MODELS_DIR"
20	
21	echo "[gpu] [1/5] miniconda (native Linux)"
22	if [ ! -x "$CONDA_DIR/bin/conda" ]; then
23	  curl -sSL https://repo.anaconda.com/miniconda/Miniconda3-latest-Linux-x86_64.sh -o /tmp/mc.sh
24	  bash /tmp/mc.sh -b -p "$CONDA_DIR" && rm -f /tmp/mc.sh
25	fi
26	# shellcheck disable=SC1091
27	source "$CONDA_DIR/etc/profile.d/conda.sh"
28	
29	# Modern conda BLOCKS non-interactive `conda create` until the defaults-channel Terms of Service
30	# are accepted (CondaToSNonInteractiveError). Accept them so the env build doesn't fail on a fresh
31	# box. (Observed on a GCP Deep-Learning-VM image, 2026-06-20.)
32	conda tos accept --override-channels --channel https://repo.anaconda.com/pkgs/main >/dev/null 2>&1 || true
33	conda tos accept --override-channels --channel https://repo.anaconda.com/pkgs/r    >/dev/null 2>&1 || true
34	
35	echo "[gpu] [2/5] WASB-SBDT repo"
36	if [ ! -d "$WASB_REPO/.git" ]; then
37	  git clone --depth 1 https://github.com/nttcom/WASB-SBDT.git "$WASB_REPO"
38	fi
39	
40	echo "[gpu] [3/5] conda env 'wasb' (py3.8 + torch 1.11.0+cu113 — the verified WASB stack)"
41	conda env list | grep -q '/envs/wasb$' || conda create -y -q -n wasb python=3.8
42	conda activate wasb
43	python -m pip install -q torch==1.11.0+cu113 torchvision==0.12.0+cu113 \
44	  --extra-index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu113
45	[ -f "$WASB_REPO/requirements.txt" ] && python -m pip install -q -r "$WASB_REPO/requirements.txt" || true
46	# WASB-SBDT's requirements.txt is INCOMPLETE: src/ imports hydra, pandas, etc. that it omits, so
47	# `import wasb_infer` deps fail without these. (Found on the GCP L4 port, 2026-06-20.) numpy is
48	# pinned <1.24 for torch-1.11 ABI compatibility.
49	python -m pip install -q hydra-core omegaconf pandas opencv-python-headless pillow scipy tqdm pyyaml matplotlib "numpy<1.24" || true
50	
51	echo "[gpu] [4/5] pretrained badminton weights"
52	WEIGHTS="$WASB_REPO/pretrained_weights/wasb_badminton_best.pth.tar"
53	if [ -f "$WEIGHTS" ]; then
54	  echo "[gpu]       found $WEIGHTS"
55	else
56	  echo "[gpu]       !! MISSING $WEIGHTS — fetch wasb_badminton_best.pth.tar (Drive/bucket) before extraction."
57	fi
58	
59	echo "[gpu] [5/5] GPU sanity check (in the wasb env)"
60	python - <<'PY'
61	import torch
62	ok = torch.cuda.is_available()
63	print("[gpu]       torch", torch.__version__, "| cuda_available", ok,
64	      "| device", (torch.cuda.get_device_name(0) if ok else "CPU"))
65	assert ok, "CUDA not available in the wasb env — check the GPU driver / base image"
66	PY
67	
68	echo "[gpu] native WASB env ready.  Activate with:  source $CONDA_DIR/etc/profile.d/conda.sh && conda activate wasb"
69	echo "[gpu] (the LinuxNativeRunner — backend/pipeline/detectors — drives extraction once it lands; M3.5.)"
70

### 135. assistant (2026-07-06T17:38:56.501Z)

> Filed from a diagnostic session (2026-07-06) where I brought up a GCP L4 VM to reproduce the "All 1 v 1.MP4" timeout. The box came up with GPU drivers but **nothing else the pipeline needs**, so setup was manual and error-prone. We should have ONE checked-in setup script that is the default bring-up template.

## Problem

There is **no checked-in setup script that brings a GCP GPU VM to a pipeline-ready state.** `deploy/gcp/create_gpu_vm.sh` creates the instance and bakes **only** `deploy/gcp/ops-agent-startup.sh` as the startup-script — that installs the **Ops Agent (observability) only**. The resulting VM has:

- ✅ NVIDIA driver + `nvidia-smi` (Deep-Learning-VM image)
- ✅ `gcloud` / `gsutil`
- ❌ **no `ffmpeg`** (and, once installed via apt, **no NVENC** → the #483 GPU transcode path can't run; you fall back to CPU libx264, ~10× slower)
- ❌ **no WASB detector env** (conda `wasb` + WASB-SBDT + torch 1.11) and **no weights** on the box
- ❌ **no docker / no `rally-worker` container** pulled

### Evidence (this session, VM `rally-diag483`, asia-southeast1-a, L4)
```
$ nvidia-smi --query-gpu=name,driver_version --format=csv,noheader
NVIDIA L4, 580.159.03            # driver OK
$ command -v docker ffmpeg gsutil python3
/snap/bin/gsutil
/usr/bin/python3                 # docker + ffmpeg ABSENT
```
Consequences: I had to `apt-get install ffmpeg` by hand (no NVENC → CPU transcode of the proxies), and I had to route **detection** through the Cloud Run Job because the box had no WASB env. A canonical setup script would have made the VM transcode-**and**-detect ready in one command.

### The recipe already exists — three times, unshared, prone to drift
1. `deploy/digitalocean/gpu_setup.sh` — a *real* bring-up script (miniconda → WASB-SBDT clone → `wasb` env torch 1.11 → deps → weights check → GPU sanity), but **DigitalOcean-specific** (`./bootstrap.sh --gpu`, DO `gpu-*-base` image) and native-only.
2. `deploy/cloudrun/Dockerfile` — the **authoritative cloud-serving** env (micromamba + conda-forge `wasb` env, same torch-1.11 stack, `WASB_PYTHON`/`WASB_REPO_DIR`/`WASB_WEIGHTS`).
3. Whatever a human types on a GCP VM by hand — **nothing checked in.**

These can (and will) drift. There is no single source of truth for "how to make a GPU box that runs this pipeline," the way `deploy/gcp/buckets.env` is the single source of truth for project/bucket names.

## Proposal

Add a **checked-in, idempotent, non-interactive** `deploy/gcp/setup_vm.sh` and make it the **default bring-up template** for GPU VMs.

### 1. `deploy/gcp/setup_vm.sh` brings a fresh GCP GPU VM to pipeline-ready
Idempotent, root-safe, logged, verifiable. It should provision:
- **NVENC-capable `ffmpeg`** (NVDEC → `scale_cuda` → NVENC), so the #483 GPU-transcode proxy path works on the box (apt ffmpeg is not enough — needs a static/nvenc build or the nv-codec-headers build, OR do transcode inside the container).
- **The WASB detector**, via ONE of (recommend **A**):
  - **A. Container path (canonical):** `gcloud auth configure-docker <region>-docker.pkg.dev` + `docker pull <region>-docker.pkg.dev/<project>/rally/rally-worker:latest`, then a thin `run_infer_local.sh` wrapper (`docker run --gpus all ... python -m backend.worker infer ...`). **Byte-identical to serving**, no env drift, already built by cloudbuild.
  - **B. Native path:** reuse the `gpu_setup.sh` / Dockerfile recipe to build the conda `wasb` env natively (for fast iteration without docker).
- **Weights** fetched to a known path from `gs://khelsutra-rally-serving/models/wasb_badminton_best.pth.tar` (matches the job's `WASB_WEIGHTS`), env exported.
- **Verification step** (`--verify`): `nvidia-smi`, `ffmpeg -hide_banner -encoders | grep -q nvenc`, and a tiny smoke detection run on a bundled 5s clip → asserts the box can transcode + detect before you rely on it.

### 2. Make it the DEFAULT (not an optional extra)
- Compose it with the existing startup-script so `create_gpu_vm.sh` yields a ready box: bake **both** `ops-agent-startup.sh` (observability) **and** `setup_vm.sh` (toolchain) via `--metadata-from-file startup-script=` (a small wrapper that sources both), OR have `create_gpu_vm.sh` print/run the one-shot `setup_vm.sh` immediately after create. Either way, the documented "one way to spin up a rally GPU box" (`create_gpu_vm.sh` header) ends at a **usable** box, not a bare one.

### 3. Single-source the WASB env recipe (stop the drift)
Factor the WASB env build into ONE shared script (e.g. `deploy/common/wasb_env.sh`) that **both** `deploy/cloudrun/Dockerfile` and `deploy/gcp/setup_vm.sh` (native path) call — analogous to `buckets.env` centralizing names. A version bump then happens in one place, not three.

## Acceptance criteria
- `bash deploy/gcp/setup_vm.sh` on a fresh `create_gpu_vm.sh` box is idempotent and finishes green; re-running is a no-op.
- `deploy/gcp/setup_vm.sh --verify` passes: driver present, `ffmpeg` has `h264_nvenc`, and a smoke `infer` on a bundled clip returns ≥1 segment.
- `create_gpu_vm.sh` (default path) produces a box where a documented one-liner transcodes a proxy **and** runs `rally-worker` detection with **no manual install**.
- WASB env recipe has a single source of truth shared by the Dockerfile and the VM setup.
- README (`deploy/gcp/README.md`) documents the bring-up → verify → teardown flow; teardown reminder unchanged (`teardown.sh delete` = $0).

## Notes / references
- Current startup-script is observability-only: `deploy/gcp/ops-agent-startup.sh` (installs Ops Agent, nothing else).
- Existing (unshared) recipes to reconcile: `deploy/digitalocean/gpu_setup.sh`, `deploy/cloudrun/Dockerfile`.
- Serving container image: `asia-southeast1-docker.pkg.dev/gen-lang-client-0306026826/rally/rally-worker:latest`; weights at `gs://khelsutra-rally-serving/models/wasb_badminton_best.pth.tar`.
- Complements #483 (its GPU transcode needs NVENC ffmpeg on the box) and #500 (chunking experiments need reproducible GPU boxes). Not blocking; devex/infra.

## Open questions
- **Container (A) vs native (B) as the default** for VM bring-up? (A = zero drift + faster to ready; B = no docker, easier to hack on `backend/` live. Could ship both, default to A.)
- Cleanest way to get **NVENC ffmpeg** on the Deep-Learning-VM image (static gpl build vs build with nv-codec-headers vs just transcode-in-container)?
- Should `setup_vm.sh` live only under `deploy/gcp/`, or should the shared `wasb_env.sh` also back the DO path (`deploy/digitalocean/gpu_setup.sh`) to fully de-dupe?

### 162. assistant (2026-07-06T18:16:48.993Z)

That's the definitive baseline — and it sharpens the story with **real measured numbers** (my ~25 fps estimate was optimistic):

## Baseline result (`8tmr7`: full original, default settings, cap removed)
- **WASB inference = 2,411.6 s** for all 46,080 frames → **~19.1 fps measured on the L4** (not 25).
- Produced **56 rally windows** — i.e. with the cap lifted, this exact video yields the highlights the alpha user should have gotten.
- **vs the 1,800 s cap: it needs 2,412 s — 34% over budget.** At the cap it got ~75% through, then was killed → 0 segments. Root cause now nailed with hard numbers (and it's worse than the 25 fps math implied, which makes downsampling clearly *necessary*, not just nice).

## Proxy math with the real 19 fps

### 170. assistant (2026-07-06T18:25:22.523Z)

# Downsample-for-detection: end-to-end resource & latency model

**Scope:** what adding the #483 GPU-transcode proxy step does to the **user's end-to-end journey** — per-stage latency + CPU/RAM/GPU footprint — vs today's no-proxy path. Grounded in real runs on the alpha clip **"All 1 v 1.MP4"** (1920×1080, 59.94 fps, 46,080 frames, 12.8 min / 768.8 s, 2.12 GiB, 23.5 Mbps H.264) on the production `rally-worker` L4 Cloud Run Job (2026-07-06).

Legend: **[M]** measured this session · **[S]** allocated spec (hard) · **[E]** estimate (flagged).

---

## TL;DR

- **Today, this clip fails.** Detection needs **2,411.6 s [M]** (46,080 frames @ **19.1 fps [M]** on L4) but WASB self-caps at **1,800 s** → it dies ~75% through with **0 segments**, even though the reel would have had **56 rallies [M]**.
- **A 720p30 proxy converts failure → success.** 30 fps halves the frames to 23,040 → detection ≈ **1,210 s [E→M pending]**, comfortably under the 1,800 s cap. The reel is still cut from the **original** (timestamps are in seconds; zero remap).
- **The transcode must be GPU (NVENC), not CPU.** CPU libx264 transcode measured **18 m 26 s [M]** — untenable, larger than the detection it's meant to shorten. GPU NVDEC→scale_cuda→NVENC is **~36 s [M, #483 dev-box]** (~10× realtime) and runs on the L4's *dedicated* NVENC/NVDEC engines, so it barely competes with detection.
- **The L4 is already paid-for and idle during detection** (<1 % GPU util [M]; #349: ~80 % idle) — detection is **CPU-decode-bound**, single-threaded. So the proxy's smaller frames cut the *actual* bottleneck (decode), and it adds **~50 % fewer GPU-seconds** overall while removing the failure mode.

---

## Allocated resources per component [S]

| Component | vCPU | RAM | GPU | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `rally-worker` (Cloud Run **Job**, per execution) | 8 | 32 GiB | 1× NVIDIA **L4** (24 GB) | task timeout 3600 s; WASB self-cap 1800 s |
| `rally-control-plane` (Cloud Run **Service**) | 2 | 8 GiB | — | min 1 always-warm (always billing) |
| GPU-transcode proxy step (proposed, same worker) | shares the 8/32/L4 | — | L4 **NVENC/NVDEC** | separate silicon from CUDA cores |

---

## End-to-end journey — latency & resource footprint

Per-stage, for **this clip**. "Current" = no proxy (fails). "With 720p30 proxy" = proposed.

| # | Stage | Current (no proxy) | With 720p30 proxy | CPU | RAM | GPU | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Upload (user → GCS) | user-bandwidth bound (2.12 GiB) | **same** (original still uploaded) | — | — | — | unchanged |
| 2 | Validate + dispatch (control plane) | ~1–3 s | ~1–3 s | low (2 vCPU) | low | — | [E] |
| 3 | Worker cold-start + GCSFuse mount | ~10–20 s | ~10–20 s | low | low | — | [M] mount ~1 s, start ~s |
| 4 | Pull source to /tmp | ~32–47 s (2.12 GiB) | ~32–47 s (pulls original) | I/O | low | — | [M] 32 s in-region |
| 5 | **Proxy transcode** (NEW) | — (n/a) | **~40–90 s** (GPU NVENC 1080p60→720p30) · CPU fallback ~18 m ✗ | 1–2 cores | ~1–2 GiB [E] | **NVENC engine** (~seconds of CUDA) | [M] GPU 36 s (#483), CPU 18m26s |
| 6 | **WASB detection** | **2,411.6 s → KILLED at 1800 s → 0 seg** | **≈1,210 s [pending]**, **< 1800 s cap ✓** | **~1–2 cores saturated (bottleneck, single-threaded decode)** | ~2–4 GiB peak [E] of 32 allocated | **<1 % util [M]** (idle; paid but unused) | [M] 19.1 fps, 56 seg |
| 7 | Reel compile (from **ORIGINAL**, full quality) | (never reached) | ~30–120 s [E] (ffmpeg trim 56 segs from original) | CPU + I/O | low | (NVENC if GPU cut) | [E] |
| 8 | Deliver reel → GCS → notify | — | ~seconds | — | — | — | [E] |
| | **TOTAL processing (stages 3–8)** | **FAILS (0 rallies to the user)** | **≈ 22–24 min → SUCCESS, 56 rallies** | | | | |

---

## The three levers, quantified [M]

| Lever | Effect on this clip | Quality risk | GPU-seconds |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A. fps 60→30 (proxy)** | 46,080 → 23,040 frames → detection **2,412 s → ~1,210 s** | golden-gate (temporal); shuttle still tracked at 30 fps | ~½ |
| **B. res 1080→720 (proxy)** | cuts the CPU **decode** cost (the real bottleneck); throughput ≥ A alone | golden-gate (spatial) | ≤ A |
| **C. batch/prefetch tuning** | GPU is <1 % utilized → raising batch/prefetch raises throughput with **identical detection** | **none** (bit-identical) | ↓ (better utilization) |

Best combined path: **720p30 proxy (A+B) + tuning (C)**, and it **composes with chunking (#500)** for arbitrarily long uploads.

---

## Cost / GPU-seconds delta (the L4 is the cost driver)

- Detection GPU-seconds: **2,412 → ~1,210** (~**50 %** reduction) [E→M pending].
- Transcode adds **~40–90** GPU-seconds on the cheap NVENC engine (not CUDA) → net still ~50 % down.
- Removes the **timeout failure mode** entirely for clips up to ~2× today's ceiling; beyond that, add #500 chunking.
- The always-warm control plane (2 vCPU/8 GiB) is unchanged by this.

---

## Honesty / provenance

- **Measured [M]:** video specs (ffprobe); baseline detection 2,411.6 s / 19.1 fps / 56 windows and the GPU-idle behavior (`rally-worker-8tmr7` logs); CPU transcode 18 m 26 s (diag VM); source pull 32 s. GPU transcode 36 s is from the #483 dev-box note, not re-measured here.
- **Pending → will be [M]:** proxy detection latency for 720p30 (`wh66x`) and 1080p30 (`kzjw6`) — expected ~1,210 s; replaces the [E] in stages 6 and the cost delta.
- **Estimates [E]:** reel-compile time, peak RAM, transcode CPU/RAM. These can be firmed up from **Cloud Monitoring / the Ops Agent** per-execution CPU/mem/GPU series — which #501's setup script would make routine. RAM is over-provisioned (32 GiB allocated vs ~2–4 GiB used [E]); a smaller memory tier may cut cost with no latency impact (verify before changing).

### 177. user (2026-07-06T18:27:09.642Z)

Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.

## Read the request first

Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page — what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in.

Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.

Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.

When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is.

Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so.

## Fundamentals for every artifact

**Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first — CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.

**Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world — its materials, instruments, vernacular — is where distinctive choices come from. Build with real content throughout, never lorem.

**Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. The Artifact CSP blocks font CDNs, so don't link a webfont URL and risk a silent fallback. Instead inline the face as a @font-face data URI. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing.

**Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject — the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited.

**Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Wide content — tables, code, diagrams — gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.

**Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly — their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults.

**Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks; visual bugs hide in the gap between source and output. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data.

**CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out — a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing.

**Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen — name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it — no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever.

**Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.

**When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number — a pill, a chip, a severity stripe — so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive.



## Process

Before writing code, sketch a short design plan — a compact token system with color, type, and layout:
- **Color**: describe the palette as 4–6 named hex values.
- **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles — a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
- **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences.

Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it.

## When the request is editorial

The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.

Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly.

**Principles** 

- The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world — headline, image, live demo, interactive moment. 
- Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content. 
- Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, a scroll-triggered reveal, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated. 
- Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
- Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

  thead th{background:var(--surface-2);font-size:11px;letter-spacing:.06em;text-transform:uppercase;color:var(--ink-2);font-weight:600;position:sticky;top:0}
  tbody tr:last-child td{border-bottom:none}
  td.num,.tnum{font-family:var(--mono);font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums;white-space:nowrap}

  .cur-fail{color:var(--crit);font-weight:600}
  .cur-ok{color:var(--ok);font-weight:600}

  /* chips */

  .m{background:var(--accent-wash);color:var(--accent-2);border-color:#bfe0e2}
  .s{background:var(--surface-2);color:var(--ink-2);border-color:var(--line)}
  .e{background:var(--warn-bg);color:var(--warn);border-color:var(--warn-line)}
  .legend{display:flex;flex-wrap:wrap;gap:14px;font-size:12.5px;color:var(--ink-2);margin-top:14px;align-items:center}

  ul.clean{margin:0;padding-left:20px} ul.clean li{margin:6px 0}
  .note{font-size:13px;color:var(--ink-2);background:var(--surface-2);border:1px solid var(--line);border-radius:12px;padding:16px 18px}
  .note b{color:var(--ink)}

  code{font-family:var(--mono);font-size:.92em;background:var(--surface-2);padding:1px 5px;border-radius:4px;border:1px solid var(--line-2)}

<div class="wrap">

  <div class="eyebrow">Diagnostic · issue #483 · alpha clip “All 1 v 1.MP4”</div>
  <h1>Downsample-for-detection: resource &amp; latency model</h1>
  <p class="dek">What adding a GPU-transcode <b>proxy step</b> does to the user’s end-to-end journey — per-stage latency and CPU / RAM / GPU footprint — measured on the production <b>L4</b> <code>rally-worker</code> job. Source clip: 1080p·59.94fps·<b>46,080 frames</b>·12.8 min·2.12 GiB.</p>

  <section>
    <div class="grid2">
      <div class="verdict v-fail">
        <div class="tag">Today · no proxy</div>
        <div class="big">Fails silently</div>
        <div class="meta">2,412 s needed · killed at 1,800 s · <b>0 rallies</b> delivered</div>
      </div>
      <div class="verdict v-ok">
        <div class="tag">With 720p30 proxy</div>
        <div class="big">Succeeds</div>
        <div class="meta">≈1,210 s detection · under cap · <b>56 rallies</b> from the original</div>
      </div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <section>
    <h2><span class="n">01</span> Detection vs the 1,800 s budget</h2>
    <p class="sub">WASB processes every frame (stride-1). Measured throughput on the L4 is <b>19.1 fps</b> — so a 12.8-min 60 fps clip overshoots the cap; halving the frame-rate to a 30 fps proxy lands it well under.</p>
    <div class="chart">
      <div class="barrow">
        <div class="lbl">Original 1080p60<span>46,080 frames · today</span></div>
        <div class="track"><div class="fill crit" style="width:92.8%"><span class="val">2,412 s → timed out</span></div><div class="cap over"></div></div>
      </div>
      <div class="barrow">
        <div class="lbl">720p30 proxy<span>23,040 frames · proposed</span></div>
        <div class="track"><div class="fill proj" style="width:46.5%"><span class="val">≈1,210 s · measuring…</span></div><div class="cap"></div></div>
      </div>
      <div class="axis"><span>0 s</span><span>1,300 s</span><span>2,600 s</span></div>
    </div>
    <div class="legend">
      <span><span class="chip m">M</span> measured this session</span>
      <span><span class="chip s">S</span> allocated spec</span>
      <span><span class="chip e">E</span> estimate / pending</span>
    </div>
  </section>

  <section>
    <h2><span class="n">02</span> End-to-end journey</h2>
    <p class="sub">Per stage, for this clip. Severity stripe marks the new step and the binding one.</p>
    <div class="scroll">
      <table>
        <thead><tr><th>Stage</th><th>Today (no proxy)</th><th>With 720p30 proxy</th><th>CPU</th><th>RAM</th><th>GPU</th></tr></thead>
        <tbody>
          <tr><td>1 · Upload → GCS</td><td class="tnum">bandwidth-bound (2.12 GiB)</td><td>same</td><td>—</td><td>—</td><td>—</td></tr>
          <tr><td>2 · Validate + dispatch</td><td class="tnum">~1–3 s</td><td class="tnum">~1–3 s</td><td>low</td><td>low</td><td>—</td></tr>
          <tr><td>3 · Cold-start + mount</td><td class="tnum">~10–20 s</td><td class="tnum">~10–20 s</td><td>low</td><td>low</td><td>—</td></tr>
          <tr><td>4 · Pull source</td><td class="tnum">~32–47 s</td><td class="tnum">~32–47 s</td><td>I/O</td><td>low</td><td>—</td></tr>
          <tr class="stripe-new"><td>5 · <b>Transcode</b> <span class="chip m">NEW</span></td><td>— n/a</td><td class="tnum"><span class="cur-ok">~40–90 s</span> GPU · <span class="cur-fail">18m26s CPU ✗</span></td><td>1–2 cores</td><td>~1–2 GiB <span class="chip e">E</span></td><td>NVENC engine</td></tr>
          <tr class="stripe-crit"><td>6 · <b>WASB detection</b></td><td class="cur-fail tnum">2,412 s → killed → 0</td><td class="cur-ok tnum">≈1,210 s ✓ <span class="pending">meas.</span></td><td>1–2 cores (bottleneck)</td><td>~2–4 GiB / 32 <span class="chip e">E</span></td><td class="cur-fail">&lt;1% util</td></tr>
          <tr><td>7 · Reel from <b>original</b></td><td>(never reached)</td><td class="tnum">~30–120 s <span class="chip e">E</span></td><td>CPU + I/O</td><td>low</td><td>opt.</td></tr>
          <tr><td>8 · Deliver + notify</td><td>—</td><td class="tnum">~seconds</td><td>—</td><td>—</td><td>—</td></tr>
          <tr style="background:var(--surface-2)"><td><b>Total (3–8)</b></td><td class="cur-fail"><b>FAILS — 0 rallies</b></td><td class="cur-ok tnum"><b>≈ 22–24 min → 56 rallies</b></td><td colspan="3" style="color:var(--ink-3)">worker: 8 vCPU · 32 GiB · 1× L4 <span class="chip s">S</span></td></tr>
        </tbody>
      </table>
    </div>
  </section>

  <section>
    <h2><span class="n">03</span> Three levers</h2>
    <div class="scroll">
      <table>
        <thead><tr><th>Lever</th><th>Effect on this clip</th><th>Quality risk</th><th>GPU-seconds</th></tr></thead>
        <tbody>
          <tr><td><b>A</b> · fps 60→30 (proxy)</td><td class="tnum">2,412 s → ~1,210 s</td><td>golden-gate (temporal)</td><td class="tnum">~½</td></tr>
          <tr><td><b>B</b> · res 1080→720 (proxy)</td><td>cuts CPU decode — the real bottleneck</td><td>golden-gate (spatial)</td><td class="tnum">≤ A</td></tr>
          <tr class="stripe-ok"><td><b>C</b> · batch/prefetch tuning</td><td>GPU is &lt;1% used → free throughput</td><td class="cur-ok">none — bit-identical</td><td class="tnum">↓ utilization</td></tr>
        </tbody>
      </table>
    </div>
    <p class="sub" style="margin-top:12px">Best path: <b>720p30 proxy (A+B) + tuning (C)</b>, and it composes with chunking (<b>#500</b>) for arbitrarily long uploads.</p>
  </section>

  <section>
    <h2><span class="n">04</span> Cost &amp; the idle-GPU finding</h2>
    <div class="grid2">
      <div class="card">
        <div class="eyebrow" style="color:var(--ink-3)">GPU-seconds / clip</div>
        <p style="margin:.5em 0 0;font-size:15px">Detection <span class="tnum">2,412 → ~1,210</span> (<b>~50% less</b>) <span class="chip e">E→M</span>. Transcode adds <span class="tnum">~40–90</span> s on the cheap NVENC engine (not CUDA). Net <b>~50% down</b> and the timeout failure mode removed.</p>
      </div>
      <div class="card">
        <div class="eyebrow" style="color:var(--ink-3)">The sharpest insight</div>
        <p style="margin:.5em 0 0;font-size:15px">The <b>L4 sits &lt;1% utilized</b> during detection <span class="chip m">M</span> — we pay for a GPU the detector barely touches; the real bottleneck is <b>single-threaded CPU decode</b>. That’s why 720p helps beyond frame-count, and why 32 GiB RAM looks over-provisioned (~2–4 GiB used).</p>
      </div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <section>
    <h2><span class="n">05</span> Provenance &amp; open gaps</h2>
    <div class="note">
      <b>Measured [M]:</b> video specs (ffprobe); baseline 2,411.6 s / 19.1 fps / 56 windows and the &lt;1% GPU behaviour (<code>rally-worker-8tmr7</code>); CPU transcode 18m26s; source pull 32 s. GPU-transcode 36 s is the #483 dev-box note, not re-measured.<br><br>
      <b>Pending → will be [M]:</b> 720p30 (<code>wh66x</code>) &amp; 1080p30 (<code>kzjw6</code>) proxy detection latency — replaces the projected bar and the two estimate cells.<br><br>
      <b>Estimates [E]:</b> reel-compile time, peak RAM, transcode CPU/RAM — firmable from Cloud Monitoring / the Ops Agent per-execution series, which <b>#501</b>’s setup script makes routine.
    </div>
  </section>

  <div class="footer">
    <span>Preview · figures finalize when proxy runs land</span>
    <span>Destination: comment on #483</span>
  </div>

### 183. assistant (2026-07-06T18:31:50.547Z)

Preview is live: **[Resource & latency model ⏱️](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/e3c49cc6-54fb-4dab-830a-1ca19aad4e3f)** — the hero bar shows the 2,412 s detection overshooting the 1,800 s cap (red) vs the 720p30 proxy landing under it (amber "measuring…", which I'll turn green with the real number when `wh66x`/`kzjw6` finish). It'll redeploy to that same URL on update.

## Why decode doesn't use the GPU today

**It can — nothing about the hardware stops it.** The L4 has a dedicated hardware video-decode block (**NVDEC**), separate silicon from the CUDA cores. The reason decode runs on CPU is a **software choice in the WASB detector, not a limitation.**

I just grepped the pipeline to confirm: *every* frame-read site — the WASB loader, all validators, person detector, motion — uses **`cv2.VideoCapture`**, which is OpenCV's **default software (CPU) decode**. There is **zero** NVDEC/`cudacodec`/`hwaccel`/cuvid anywhere. See [wasb_infer.py:758-772](../khelsutra-guru/badminton-highlight-indexer/backend/pipeline/detectors/wasb_infer.py) — it opens a `cv2.VideoCapture` and pulls frames, then converts BGR→PIL→tensor **on the CPU** before the one GPU forward pass. That CPU decode+convert path, single-threaded, *is* the ~19 fps ceiling — and it's why the GPU sits <1%.

**What it would take to move decode onto the GPU** (why it's not just a flag):
1. **A CUDA decode library in the env** — OpenCV built with CUDA (`cv2.cudacodec.VideoReader`), or ffmpeg `-hwaccel cuda`/`h264_cuvid`, or NVIDIA DALI / PyNvVideoCodec / `torchvision` GPU reader / `decord(gpu)`. Stock OpenCV wheels are CPU-only.
2. **Fit the pinned WASB stack** (torch 1.11.0+cu113, py3.8) — this is the real friction: ABI/version compatibility for a GPU-decode lib in that frozen env.
3. **Rewrite the frame loader** to keep data on-GPU: NVDEC yields NV12/YUV → color-convert + resize to the fixed net input **on the GPU** → feed as a CUDA tensor, skipping the CPU round-trip. Today it's BGR→PIL→ToTensor on CPU.
4. **Parity check** — hardware decode can differ subtly from software (chroma handling), and WASB was tuned on CPU-decoded frames, so it needs the same golden-gate as the proxy to confirm detection doesn't shift.

**Why it matters:** NVDEC on an L4 decodes 1080p H.264 at *hundreds* of fps. It attacks the actual bottleneck head-on — potentially taking throughput far past 19 fps and making the L4 you're already paying for earn its keep. It could even remove the need to downsample for many videos.

**This is already a known lever — issue #349** tracks exactly this ("WASB decode is CPU-bound/single-threaded → L4 ~80% idle"), and today's diagnosis is fresh evidence for it.

**How it relates to the other fixes** (they compose, they don't compete):
- The **proxy (#483) reduces the *number* of frames**; **NVDEC makes each frame *cheap to decode*.** One ironic note: the proxy's *transcode* step already uses GPU NVDEC→NVENC — so we'd be GPU-decoding to make the proxy, then CPU-decoding that proxy for detection. NVDEC in detection closes that gap.
- **Tuning (lever C)** only *hides* CPU-decode latency by overlapping it with GPU work; **NVDEC removes it.**

**Bottom line:** GPU decode is the highest-ceiling fix but the highest-effort (env packaging + loader rewrite + parity gate = #349). The proxy and batch/prefetch tuning are lower-effort wins that ship sooner; NVDEC is the one that makes the idle L4 actually pay off. Want me to write this up as a comment on **#349** with the measured `<1% GPU / 19 fps` evidence, so the lever is documented alongside the others?

#349 [OPEN] WASB native: decode is CPU-bound & single-threaded in streaming mode → L4 GPU ~80% idle (no NVDEC/parallel decode)  labels=

### 188. assistant (2026-07-06T18:43:16.002Z)

The proxy runs finished — and the results are decisive:

| Variant | Frames | WASB detect | Throughput | Rallies | vs 1,800 s cap |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Original 1080p60 (cap lifted) | 46,080 | 2,411.6 s | 19.1 fps | 56 | ✗ times out (34% over) |
| **720p30 proxy** | 23,040 | **955.9 s** | 24.1 fps | **60** | ✅ **47% under** |
| 1080p30 proxy | 23,040 | 1,301.8 s | 17.7 fps | 57 | ✅ 28% under |

**"Would have surely processed" is now proven** — 720p30 finishes in 956 s (47% under the real cap) with 60 rallies; quality is stable (56→57→60). And a bonus: 720p30 vs 1080p30 at *identical* 23,040 frames is **36% faster purely from smaller frames** — hard proof the pipeline is **decode-bound**, which is exactly #349 (title confirmed: *"decode is CPU-bound & single-threaded → L4 ~80% idle → no NVDEC"*).

Executing all four asks. First, writing the two GitHub comments and the updated deliverable:

### 189. assistant (2026-07-06T18:43:31.765Z)

## Fresh measured evidence — decode is the bottleneck, GPU (NVDEC) is idle

From a diagnostic session (2026-07-06) on the alpha clip that timed out — **"All 1 v 1.MP4"** (1080p60, 46,080 frames, 12.8 min) — all runs on the production `rally-worker` L4:

**The clincher for "decode-bound, not compute-bound":** two proxies with **identical frame counts** (23,040) but different resolution —

| Variant | Frames | WASB detect | Throughput |
|---|---|---|---|
| 720p30 | 23,040 | 955.9 s | **24.1 fps** |
| 1080p30 | 23,040 | 1,301.8 s | **17.7 fps** |

Same frames, **+36 % throughput purely from smaller frames** → cost scales with **pixels decoded on the CPU**, not with the (fixed-input) GPU forward pass. Baseline full 1080p60: 2,411.6 s for 46,080 frames = **19.1 fps** with the **L4 <1 % utilized** — matches this issue's "~80 % idle".

**Why decode is on CPU today (software choice, not hardware):** every frame-read in the pipeline is `cv2.VideoCapture` = OpenCV **software** decode. Grepped the repo — **no** `cudacodec`/`hwaccel`/NVDEC/`cuvid` anywhere: `backend/pipeline/detectors/wasb_infer.py:758-772` (the WASB streaming loader), plus all validators, `person_detector`, `motion`. Frames go BGR→PIL→ToTensor on CPU, then a single GPU forward pass. The L4's NVDEC block sits idle.

**What moving decode to NVDEC takes:**
1. A CUDA decode path in the pinned WASB env (torch 1.11.0+cu113 / py3.8): OpenCV-CUDA `cv2.cudacodec.VideoReader`, ffmpeg `-hwaccel cuda`/`h264_cuvid`, or DALI / PyNvVideoCodec / torchvision GPU reader. Stock OpenCV wheels are CPU-only — **env packaging is the main friction**.
2. Rewrite the frame loader to stay on-GPU: NVDEC (NV12/YUV) → GPU color-convert + resize to the fixed net input → CUDA tensor, skipping the CPU round-trip (`make_video_frame_loader` / `frame_to_tensor` in `wasb_infer.py`).
3. **Parity gate:** hardware vs software decode can differ subtly (chroma); WASB is tuned on CPU-decoded frames → golden-set F1/tolF1 check before flipping (same gate class as #483).

**Payoff:** NVDEC on an L4 decodes 1080p H.264 at *hundreds* of fps → could take throughput well past 19 fps and make the idle L4 pay off, potentially removing the need to downsample for many clips.

**How it composes with the other levers:**
- **#483 (proxy)** reduces the *number* of frames; **NVDEC** makes each frame *cheap to decode* — complementary. (Note the proxy's transcode already uses NVDEC→NVENC; we then CPU-decode the proxy. NVDEC in detection closes that gap.)
- **batch/prefetch tuning** only *overlaps* CPU decode; NVDEC *removes* it.
- **#500 (chunking)** cuts wall-clock; NVDEC cuts per-frame cost.

Highest-effort of the levers (env + loader rewrite + parity gate), **highest ceiling** — the only one that makes the GPU do the decode work it's currently sitting idle for. A dedicated tracked project is being spun up to execute this behind a default-OFF flag.
